DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
Executive summary
Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
A record 1.57 million international passengers arrived at Cape Town International in
FY25/26, against 1.16 million in FY19/20, before the pandemic. Its load factor of 79% across
all its seats in 2025 was the joint highest of South Africa's five largest airports, with Lanseria.
Between 2015 and 2025 the airport added 17 mainline international routes, more than any of
eight peer airports. On the airport count, the recovery is complete. The next stage is harder,
because the system that delivered it is now full at the peak.
Interest is not what holds the province back. About 97 million high-value travellers in 34 markets are
interested in South Africa, yet only about one in seventy of them, some 1.4 million, turns that interest into
a trip. Seats and bookings stand in the way. Eleven of 27 international routes run above 80% load across
the year, and at the summer peak one runway caps movements at about 30 an hour. In winter, carriers
pull seats: June holds 6.4% of the year's international capacity, against 10.8% in December. Beyond the
Metro, most rural properties are missing from the online travel agents where visitors book.
On the growth model's count, arrivals can grow from about 1.5 million in 2025 to 2.5 million by 2035, if the
province wins the seats. That makes the action plan a supply programme before it is a marketing
programme.
The province earns a premium from a narrow base
The Western Cape earns more per visitor than the rest of South Africa. The province takes about a
quarter of national foreign spend from about a seventh of the arrivals. Its foreign visitors spend roughly 1.6
times the national average. That is about US$890 per arrival, against US$500 for the rest of South Africa.
Foreign direct spend rose 14% in a year, from R22.7bn in FY2024 to R25.9bn in FY2025.
Yet the premium rests on a few markets, six months and one metro. Five markets supply 63% of overseas
arrivals, and the UK, Germany and the USA alone 52%. More than half the arrivals on the ten largest
routes come through someone else's hub: the routes through Dubai, Doha, Addis Ababa and Istanbul
carry 53% of them. October to March carries between 61% and 62% of overseas visitor travel. The Cape
Metro holds 20,281 of the province's 50,332 tourism assets, or 40.3%, and visitors disperse from there.
Growth now needs new markets, new seasons and new regions.
The limits are physical and institutional
Five constraints cap the case, and none of them is a lack of interest in the province. [Summary of
Findings, 14 Oct 2026, slides 3 and 4: align these five constraints with the deck's own list once the deck is
in hand.]
The gateway. The growth model draws air access as a line at 1.8 million inbound seats, and the base
path crosses it around 2030. The line is the model's assumption, not a measured cap. In practice the
runway binds first, at peak hours. Runway relief comes from about 2033. Airports Company South Africa
(ACSA) now schedules the realigned runway for December 2029, subject to contract award. The plan's
phasing assumes about 2033 and counts the earlier date as upside. George Airport is domestic only, and
Cape Winelands Airport targets 2028 with no named carrier.
Full routes. Five of the eleven full routes are mature long-haul services, led by Newark at 88.6%, Atlanta
at 84.3% and Frankfurt and London Heathrow at 83.0%. On full routes, carriers are expected to raise fares
before they add seats.
Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory 11
Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
Seasons. The green season, April to September, is a seat problem and a demand problem at once: seats
and visitors fall away together. Connecting traffic is "especially critical in the low season". That ties winter
seats to the hub carriers' own priorities.
Reach. Accessibility falls from 4.7 out of 5 in the Cape Metro to 1.0 in the Central Karoo, and one touring
route of six is rated Ready. Beyond the Metro and the Garden Route the province is effectively car-only.
Levers held elsewhere. National government and its state-owned entities hold visas, air service
agreements and the airport and port programmes. Mobility mandates are split across three tiers, and no
approved method exists for provincial tourism GDP and jobs.
Most of the five sit outside a marketing budget, and the runway and air rights sit outside the province.
What 2.5 million arrivals would take
Wesgro's original ask was larger: to "double international tourist arrivals to the Western Cape by 2035".
The evidence reframes that ambition as growth to 2.5 million. The province's Growth for Jobs Strategy
sets 3 million by 2035 on South African Tourism's count. That sits above every path the growth model
draws, and section 1.1 explains why the two cannot yet be compared directly. [G4J 2023 document to be
read and the 3 million target confirmed against it.]
The gateway sets the pace in the summer peak, and demand and conversion set it in the green season
and beyond the Metro. The growth model draws three paths from a 2025 base of about 1.5 million. By
2035 the conservative path reaches about 1.8 million, the base path about 2.2 million and the accelerated
path about 2.5 million. Chapters 1 to 5 call the last two the base and accelerated cases.
The paths differ by intervention, not by demand potential. The base path assumes Wesgro holds its
current effort while Emirates, Qatar, Ethiopian and Turkish Airlines keep adding capacity. The accelerated
path also needs direct India and China routes and dedicated activation in India, China, the USA and the
Gulf. Without confirmed routes, it reverts to base.
Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory 12
Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
Figure ES.1. Arrivals paths to 2035
Source: Market Assessment Report, May 2026, p19 (2025 base); Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p20, p21. The 2031 base value and the
accelerated and conservative milestones are computed (section 5.3).
On the eleven full routes, marketing cannot make up for missing seats, but on the Dubai, Addis Ababa and
Doha routes, at 65% to 72% load, it can. The hub markets supply about 30% of the arrivals the base path
adds, the largest share of any market group.
The base path implies growth of 3.8% a year and the accelerated path 5.3%, against a global norm of 3%
to 5%. Faster growth has been sustained outside a recovery. Santiago's airport grew from about 16 million
passengers in 2010 to more than 26 million in 2019, at least 5.5% a year. Chile's international seat
capacity rose about 40% to 50% from 2013 to 2019. The fastest growers aimed institutions, marketing and
air access at one binding constraint. For Chile that constraint was long-haul access. The Western Cape's
constraint has the same shape: seats into one gateway airport.
The action plan makes three shifts
In Pillar 1, Reshape demand, the work moves from awareness to conversion. The province is under-sold
in its growth markets and hard to book outside its core. Three of its six focus areas aim at conversion:
green-season events, bookable product and hub traffic already in the air. About 101,000 Indian and
Chinese travellers a year already reach Cape Town, mainly through Gulf and African hubs. Singapore
shows how a hub can turn transit into visits, and Melbourne uses its events calendar to steady demand.
In Pillar 2, Transform supply, the work moves from new attractions to seats and finished experiences. The
pillar has one capacity entry: frequency and larger aircraft on the full routes ahead of runway relief.
Following Chile, Initiative I6 designs a public-private air access fund in 2027 and 2028 to back it (section
4.3). Five more cover cruise, reach, product and experience. Cruise grows through ships and excursions
rather than port capital. At the flagship sites the task is to manage flows, because Boulders is near
carrying capacity and Cableway queues run up to three hours. Elsewhere it is to turn existing assets into
complete visitor experiences.
Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory 13
Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
In Pillar 3, Build enabling capability, the work moves from separate mandates to shared decisions, capital
and data. The pillar answers four questions. First, who benefits: twenty township and rural communities
hold 18.4% of the province's residents, but the spend traced to them is a floor of 0.02% to 0.04% of
foreign direct spend. Second, who pays: the capital pipeline funds airports, ports, roads and rail, but
almost none of it reaches the beds, booking and product the regions lack. Third, who decides: with visas
and air service agreements held nationally, the province leads by escalation. Fourth, who measures: more
than twenty bodies produce tourism data, yet no one holds a single view of visitor behaviour. The New
Zealand case argues for yield over arrivals, so the plan tracks spend, season and spread beside arrivals
(section 5.3).
The three pillars make one case. Demand work fills seats only if the seats exist, and supply work pays
only if the product can be booked. Both need the enabling base to last.
Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory 14
Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
Twenty focus areas at a glance
The research rests on a hypothesis tree of three pillars, six themes and twenty focus areas (section 1.4).
Each focus area names a lever on growth, framed so the evidence could test it. Nineteen were tested
against the evidence of a named workstream. The evidence supports all nineteen, nine strongly and ten
with a gap or a qualifier (Appendix A1). The twentieth, workforce and service skills, waits on a labour
analysis still to be commissioned.

Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory 17
Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
Seats and bookings move first
Air access and bookability move first, because every other lever runs through them. The plan runs in three
phases. Activate, from 2027 to 2028, holds and adds seats on the full routes, counts the bookability gap
and sets up the institutions the rest depends on. Gate 1 closes the phase at the end of 2028, two years
before the base path crosses the seat line. Expand, from 2029 to 2031, scales what works and meets the
seat line at the 2030 mid-term review. Sustain, from 2032 to 2035, takes direct route cases to carriers as
runway capacity arrives.
Eighteen initiatives carry the plan, one per focus area except where one owner delivers two. Wesgro is
accountable for twelve. The Department of Economic Development and Tourism (DEDAT), the
Department of Mobility, municipalities, local tourism organisations (LTOs) and site operators lead the other
six. Thirteen draw mainly on Wesgro's operating budget. None has yet been costed, and setting the rand
bands is the first funding task (section 4.6).
Twelve actions in three strands can start in the first 24 months, nine of them inside Wesgro's current
mandate and budget.
Seats and season come first, from the first half of 2027. Cape Town Air Access holds winter seats through
joint marketing with carriers on the full routes and tracks the runway quarterly (I6, I2). The Convention
Bureau publishes a green-season events calendar and aims bids at May to September (I2).
Governance and baselines also start then. The Wesgro CEO's office and DEDAT constitute the Tourism
Growth Steerco and table the first national escalation agenda (I15, I17). Wesgro's Research unit agrees a
data-sharing protocol with ACSA, SA Tourism, Stats SA and Cape Town Tourism (I16). It also runs a
bookability census by district and requests visitor counts from the five flagship sites (I3, I11).
Product and inclusion follow in the second half of 2027. Wesgro's Tourism unit packages the Garden
Route and Route 62 with a shuttle transfer (I8, I9). It also packages the Southern Line as a visitor product
with the City of Cape Town and the Passenger Rail Agency of South Africa (I8). It chooses two or three
pilot communities, and from the first half of 2028 the LTOs give each community one live booking route
before any promotion (I12, I3). Each action opens a seat, a baseline or a booking path a later initiative
needs. Owners and start dates are proposals for Steerco to confirm (section 4.5).
How the Board will track progress
The Board will judge the plan on seven impact indicators, and today only arrivals and spend carry a
modelled value for 2035. Beneath them sit twenty-five outcome and thirty-six output indicators. Steerco
receives a quarterly scorecard and the Board an annual outlook against both paths. Gates at the end of
2028 and 2031, a mid-term review in 2030 and a terminal evaluation in 2035 decide what each phase
funds.

The table shows the model's arrivals baseline, and the airport count is tracked beside it, from 1.44 million
in FY24/25 (section 5.3). The spend target needs the same care. The model's 2024 spend of R27.12bn
sits 19% above the R22.7bn reported for FY2024, so R40.6bn is not measured from R25.9bn. [Open item:
Skift to rebase the spend path on the FY2025 actual of R25.9bn.]
Jobs wait on the labour analysis, so the ambition of 300,000 tourism-linked jobs is tested before it
becomes a target. The green-season share starts at 38.1%, but the share outside the Metro has no
baseline until the data protocol delivers one in 2027 (section 5.3). Gate 1 turns the missing baselines into
targets, so the first two years must produce data as well as results.
What could knock it off course
Ten of the fourteen material risks sit outside Wesgro's control, so the response to most is early warning
and a route to the tier that can act. One is rated high on both likelihood and impact: restrictive bilateral air
agreements and the US carrier cap. It calls for an escalation agenda with stated asks, plus route work
inside existing rights.
Six are rated medium likelihood and high impact, in three pairs; first, two sit with the national tier and
ACSA. Runway relief may slip past about 2033, so its milestones are tracked quarterly. Visa processing
may not improve, and the escalation agenda carries it. Second, two sit in the markets. A Gulf hub
disruption would cut connecting capacity, so hub work is spread across all four hubs. A downturn in the
UK, Germany and the USA would hit 52% of overseas arrivals, so activation shifts towards Africa and the
hub markets. Third, two sit closer to delivery. Safety incidents could deter visitors, so safety gates come
before any pilot launch. A cut to Wesgro's operating budget would hit thirteen initiatives, so rand bands
and a stop list are due before Gate 1 (section 4.6).
Two more are rated high likelihood and medium impact. Climate events that close sites and routes call for
a climate risk register. The loss of LTO funding, which MFMA Circular No. 131 has disrupted, calls for
stable funding agreements with a development mandate. Likelihood and impact are Letsema's
assessment, and Steerco reviews the register each quarter (section 4.8).
Several items remain open, from page references still to confirm to the source of the 250-call cruise target and a
provincial employment baseline. Each is marked in square brackets where it occurs and listed with an owner in
the accompanying open-items register. The chapters that follow give the source of every figure in this summary.

DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
Chapter 1
Context
Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
1.1 Mandate and objective
Wesgro commissioned research and an action plan to "double international tourist arrivals to
the Western Cape by 2035". The evidence reframes that goal as growth to 2.5 million
arrivals, paced by gateway capacity in the summer peak and by demand in the green
season.1
Wesgro acts through partners to create jobs
Wesgro promotes tourism, trade and investment for Cape Town and the Western Cape, so the tourism
goal sits inside a wider economic brief. Its mission is to work "together with partners, drive inclusive and
sustainable economic growth, strengthen competitiveness, and create jobs in Cape Town and the
Western Cape". Its vision is to "be a leading regional tourism, trade and investment promotion agency".
Two points in the mission shape the recommendations: growth is a means to jobs, and Wesgro acts
through partners rather than holding the levers itself.2
Four of Wesgro's eight strategy statements bear directly on this study. The first sets the destination
proposition: Cape Town and the Western Cape "top of mind as a leading business and tourism destination
with premium products and services". The fourth names new markets and buyers in China, ASEAN, India,
Brazil and the Middle East, "while maintaining a continued focus on established markets". The sixth
commits Wesgro to "Track and address systemic challenges influencing economic competitiveness", and
the seventh to "Provide economic data, insights & analytics for the Western Cape". The growth-market
work in focus area 4 centres on two of them: India and China, whose travellers already reach Cape Town
through Gulf and African hubs. The growth model's acceleration group holds the Gulf, China, India and
Brazil.3
Of four ambitions, only arrivals has a target the evidence supports
The objective, set in November 2025, is "To develop an evidence-based tourism strategy that will double
international tourist arrivals to the Western Cape by 2035". It continues: "through a holistic approach that
drives demand, strengthens supply and enables inclusive, sustainable and geographically dispersed
growth aligned with WESGRO's strategic priorities". The research builds the evidence the objective calls
for, and Chapters 3 and 4 turn it into recommendations and initiatives. The objective fixes the three pillars
the report keeps throughout: demand, supply and enablement. Of its terms, only the doubling did not
survive the evidence.4
Four headline ambitions sat under the objective: 2.8 million international visitors, 300,000 tourism-linked
jobs, 10,000 SMMEs onboarded and twice the airlift and cruise volumes. The report carries all four
forward. The evidence reframed the first and changed how the other three are measured.5
Arrivals. The growth model tested the doubling and supports 2.2 million to 2.5 million by 2035. From
1,498,829 arrivals in 2025, its base case reaches 2,175,778 by 2035 and its accelerated case 2,516,282,
above a conservative floor of 1,822,634. The base case holds Wesgro's effort at 2025 intensity, with
selective frequency gains and continued hub expansion. The accelerated case needs confirmed direct
routes to India and China, above-baseline activation and more hub capacity into Cape Town; without the
routes, volume reverts to base. Growth to 2.5 million is that case rounded, and it is the goal the report
carries.6

Even the base case outruns the model's air access. The model draws a seat line at 1.8 million inbound
seats, and the base case first passes it at the 2030 year point, at 1,802,929 arrivals. The line is an
assumption rather than a measured cap. In practice the runway binds first, at peak hours (section 1.3).
Growth to 2.5 million is therefore a supply programme before it is a marketing programme.7
Jobs. The ambition was 300,000 tourism-linked jobs, yet the evidence holds no Western Cape tourism
employment figure (section 2.4). Jobs therefore enter Chapter 5 without a baseline. Wesgro and DEDAT
should commission a labour analysis in 2027 to set one (focus area 20).
SMMEs. The ambition was 10,000 SMMEs onboarded, with no baseline or definition printed [Wesgro to
confirm the baseline count and definition of SMMEs onboarded]. The action plan treats it as community
inclusion, measured in visitor spend reaching priority communities rather than a count of firms (section 2.4
and Chapter 5).
Airlift and cruise. The ambition was twice the airlift and cruise volumes. Section 3.2.1 sets airlift as the
seats and routes needed to lift the model's 1.8 million seat line. It sets cruise as calls and ports [Cruise
Cape Town to confirm the call target and its definition]. The cruise baseline is 79 vessels in the 2024/25
season. Of the four ambitions, only arrivals has a target the evidence supports today. The other three go
forward with their baselines still to be fixed.8
Wesgro's arrivals metric fits a ten-year reading
Wesgro measures itself against six strategic outcomes, each with one metric. Two bear on tourism:
800,000 to 1 million new international tourist arrivals and 161 business event bids secured, worth an
estimated R4.5 billion. None of the six states a period [Wesgro to confirm the period over which the six
outcome metrics are measured].9
The period decides how the arrivals metric compares with the growth case. Over ten years, 800,000 to 1
million new arrivals sits between the two paths, which add 676,949 and 1,017,453 from 2025 to 2035. Yet
over the five years to 2030 it is more than twice what the base case adds by then, about 304,000.10
The business-events metric ties to the plan's green-season focus (focus area 2). International association
meetings in Cape Town rose from 33 in 2022 to 58 in 2024 and are "primarily hosted between May and
November". That window overlaps the green season, April to September, but 74 of the 136 meetings from
2022 to 2024 fell between September and November. Bids must therefore pull meetings into the trough
months (section 3.1). Chapter 5 tracks the nearest indicators: international arrivals and association
meetings in Cape Town (T1-01 and T2-02).11
The Growth for Jobs target sits above both paths
The study's contract requires this report and its recommendations to align with two documents: the Growth
for Jobs Strategy (G4J) and Wesgro's FY26 to FY30 plan. This report is the Final Research Report under
clause 1.1.14, and Chapter 5 answers the monitoring and evaluation clauses 1.1.4 and 1.1.10 [clause
references to be checked against the signed SLA Annexure B, whose wording is not in hand].12
The Growth for Jobs Strategy 2023, approved by Cabinet, designates tourism a primary tradable sector
for the province. Its arrivals target is 3 million international tourist arrivals by 2035 on the South African
Tourism basis. [G4J 2023 document to be read and the 3 million target confirmed against it.] On that basis
the province received 1,538,142 in 2025, still below the 2 million of 2019 on which G4J builds. The 3
million sits above both of the model's paths. Part of the gap is method: G4J adds 1 million to the 2019

How the report counts arrivals
Four kinds of arrivals figure circulate around this study: ambitions, airport passengers, South African
Tourism's tourist arrivals and the model's arrivals. Appendix A2 lists all eight with their bases. The report
keeps them apart under three rules. Airport passenger arrivals at Cape Town International, by fiscal year,
are the baseline and tracking series, with FY24/25 at 1.44 million. The model's 2025 base of 1,498,829
and its 2035 points are the target series. [Open item: Skift to confirm in writing what the model counts (SA
Tourism tourist arrivals, ACSA passengers including returning residents or OAG origin-and-destination
flows) and how the Hubs group is built.] South African Tourism's provincial count is used once, to read
across to the G4J target. The report quotes the series side by side and never adds one to the other [model
owner to confirm the series and year basis behind the 2025 base of 1,498,829].15
1.2 The Western Cape tourism system today
International arrivals at Cape Town International have recovered to a record, and the system
that delivered the recovery runs through five source markets, six summer months and one
metro.
Arrivals and recovery
International passenger arrivals at Cape Town International reached 1.44m in FY24/25, against 1.16m in
FY19/20, the last pre-pandemic year. Arrivals passed the pre-pandemic peak of 1.19m, set in FY18/19,
during FY23/24, when they reached 1.36m. FY25/26 added a further step to 1.57m.16
The fall and the climb were both steep. Arrivals dropped to 80,400 in FY20/21 and 378,500 in FY21/22,
then returned to 1.07m in FY22/23. Two-way international passengers reached 2.90m in FY24/25, 29%
above FY17/18, and 3.3m in calendar 2025 inside a total of 11.1m, both up 7% on the year.17
Cape Town's recovery also ran ahead of the national one, so the destination gained share as it recovered.
In the first half of 2024 its air arrivals had recovered further against 2019 than South Africa's in eight of the
ten largest markets. The USA stood at 162% and Australia at 172% of their 2019 level.18

Spend and value
Foreign direct spend in the province rose from R22.7bn in FY2024 to R25.9bn in FY2025, a gain of 14% in
one year. The Western Cape earns that spend from a modest share of the national market. Across the
four quarters of 2025 it took 16%, 13%, 13% and 16% of South Africa's foreign arrivals and 26%, 20%,
22% and 27% of foreign direct spend.19
The gap between the two shares is the province's premium. About a quarter of national spend from about
a seventh of arrivals means the average foreign visitor to the Western Cape spends roughly 1.6 times the
national average. The province earns about US$890 per international arrival, against US$500 for the rest
of South Africa and US$630 for Morocco, though below Kenya's US$1,460.20
More than a quarter of overseas visitors stay between 7 and 21 days, on printed bands that sum to only
48%. Some 5% stay 1 to 2 days, 12% 3 to 4, 14% 7 to 10, 3% 11 to 14, 13% 15 to 21 and 1% other. The
2024 Wesgro Visitor Tracking Survey gives the same distribution, and 52.33% of its respondents gave no
length of stay, so the bands describe about half the sample.21
The modal visitor in the 2024 Wesgro survey spends R1,000 to R2,000 a day, is aged 36 to 50 and travels
as a pair. Nearly 40% spend more than R1,000 a day. Self-catering at 17.4% and guesthouses at 9.7%
lead accommodation choice, ahead of hotels at 3.6%; together these shares cover under a third of
respondents.22
Source markets
Five markets supply 63% of overseas arrivals. In 2025 the UK held 19%, Germany 17%, the USA 16%,
the Netherlands 7% and France 4%, and the three largest alone held 52%. The report uses 63%, the sum
of the printed shares.23

Concentration is not new. On the Cape Town overseas air-arrivals basis for January to August 2024 the
same five markets held 61%, with the UK and the USA at 18% each and Germany at 15%. The growth
markets remain small: India supplied about 2.6% of South Africa's overseas tourists in September 2025,
the Middle East 2.3% and China 1.4%.24
The mature markets also pay the most. Average spend per visitor in 2024 ran from R32,900 for the USA,
R26,500 for the UK and R25,600 for Germany down to R8,600 for Namibia and R8,500 for Zimbabwe.
The spread is 3.9 times, and the two regional markets compensate with stability, repeat demand and
counter-seasonal volume.25
Most of these visitors arrive through someone else's hub. The UAE route carried 222,923 air arrivals into
Cape Town in 2025, the UK 158,448, Qatar 126,078, Ethiopia 123,250 and Turkiye 89,090. The four hub
routes through Dubai, Doha, Addis Ababa and Istanbul carried 53% of arrivals on the ten largest routes,
so the hubs, not the source markets, are the province's working gateways.26

Seasonality
Demand peaks in the southern summer. In 2025, 12.7% of overseas visitors travelled in December, 11.5%
in January and 11.3% in November, against 6.1% in June and 6.5% in August. October to March together
account for about 62% of the year once the twelve printed shares, which sum to 104.9%, are scaled to
100% [Wesgro Research to confirm the 2025 month-of-travel shares].27
The 2024 Wesgro Visitor Tracking Survey, whose monthly shares sum to 100%, gives 61% for October to
March and 39% for April to September.28
Supply follows the same curve. June carries 6.4% of 2025 international seat capacity into Cape Town, and
Cape Town hotel occupancy was 53% in June 2023. [Open item: Wesgro Research to supply June 2025
occupancy.] The conclusion follows: growth "will depend on improving conversion in Apr-Sep rather than
pushing incremental demand into already busy peak months".29

Air and sea access
Ten airlines fly to Cape Town International from Africa, nine from Europe, three from North and South
America, two from the Middle East and one, Singapore Airlines, from Asia. None flies nonstop between
Cape Town and Asia, so the gap is "the absence of any direct capacity to any of the Asian markets", India
and China included. Twenty-nine international routes serve the airport in 2026.30
Cape Town International itself is now the physical ceiling. One runway with no parallel taxiway caps
movements at about 30 an hour, and ACSA's programme of about R11.3bn brings relief from about 2032
for the international terminal and about 2033 for the runway. ACSA's latest schedule sets December 2026
to December 2029 for the realigned runway, subject to contract award. The secondary gateways do not
change the picture. George, above its design limit at 911,000 passengers in 2025, doubles by 2028 or
2029, with construction from October 2026. Cape Winelands Airport targets 2028 with no named carrier.31
Cruise has grown without new berth capacity. The province hosted 70, 67 and 79 vessels in the 2022/23,
2023/24 and 2024/25 seasons, carrying 144,125, 109,884 and 127,178 passengers. The Cape Town
berth took 83 calls in 2024/25, with 127,000 passengers and R1.79bn of economic impact. [StratEcon
2024/25 cruise report to confirm whether 79 vessels and 83 calls differ by definition.]32
Port reliability, not berth space, is the risk. The berth has room for 150 or more calls a season, and its
limits are a 300m ship length and the South-Easter wind. The Port of Cape Town is "a binding constraint,
with congestion and equipment failures affecting export and cruise reliability".33
Geography of the system
The Cape Metro generates 72.5% of provincial GDPR, a whole-economy share rather than a tourism one.
The Cape Winelands follows at 11.2%, the Garden Route at 7.6%, the West Coast at 4.8%, the Overberg
at 3% and the Central Karoo at 0.5%. Only the Garden Route share is dated, to 2023, while the Metro
chart is titled 2024 and the rest carry no year.34
Tourism supply is concentrated in the same way. The province holds 50,332 mapped tourism assets
across the six regions, of which the Metro holds 20,281, or 40.3%. The Garden Route holds 9,875, the
Cape Winelands 7,730, the Overberg 6,176, the West Coast 4,752 and the Central Karoo 1,518.35
Cape Town is the axle of the system, "the province's global shopfront and primary dispersal hub". Any
capacity failure in Cape Town "quickly becomes a provincial risk, not just a city problem". International
visitors land there and disperse from there, and readiness falls with each step away: accessibility scores
drop from 4.7 in the Metro to 1.0 in the Central Karoo.36
Beyond the Metro the visitor drives. The N2 is "the only continuous inter-regional tourism corridor, creating
a single point of failure", and large parts of the province outside the Metro and the Garden Route remain
effectively car-only destinations. The further from Cape Town, the faster the infrastructure falls away.37

Rail, roads and utilities
Passenger rail has collapsed as a mode. Trainsets fell from about 110 in 1995 to about 91, pushing some
400,000 commuters onto saturated roads, and congestion in the Metro costs an estimated R2.8bn a year.
Metrorail still runs 460km, five lines and 104 of 121 stations, with 22.7m passenger trips in FY2025, but no
line reaches the airport and it is rated functionally unusable for tourism.38
Utilities cap the periphery before demand does. Water scarcity in high-growth nodes such as the Garden
Route acts as a "hard ceiling" for visitor load, and load shedding raises costs and degrades the
experience in peripheral districts. The airport has no fuel pipeline and depends on road tankers, and the
2017 to 2018 water crisis left a lasting perception of resource pressure.39
The remedies are named but not scheduled. Four transport enablers are listed, none dated or costed: the
Cape Town Integrator, rail restoration and devolution, a high-speed city to airport link and George to Cape
Winelands passenger rail. PRASA's R21.1bn programme and the City's R123bn, 30-year devolution case
cover fleet and lines, and neither carries a tourism product or an airport link.40
Institutions
Wesgro runs the demand side of this system through six units: Cape Town Air Access, Cruise Cape
Town, the Convention Bureau, Destination Marketing, Research and Tourism. Around them sit fifteen
external organisations across three spheres of government and industry.41
The levers that bind the system sit with those partners. National government and its agencies, among
them South African Tourism, ACSA and Transnet, hold visa rules, air service agreements, route rights and
airport and port operations. The province holds jobs, inclusion and spatial outcomes, sector development,
safety programmes and infrastructure planning. The City delivers services, manages precincts and
readies events. Industry, from Cape Town Tourism, the V&A Waterfront and the flagship sites to the cruise
lines and hotels, holds route viability, demand generation and product standards.42
Alignment across those tiers is thin. Stakeholders described strain at SA Tourism and a lack of fiscal
space. More than twenty bodies produce tourism data and "there is no single view of visitor behaviour,
making strategy reactive rather than predictive". Fifteen of the sixteen organisations interviewed raised
38

industry and government collaboration, and the Department of Economic Development named restrictive
bilateral air agreements and below-peer destination funding as high-impact constraints.43
None of these constraints is a shortage of visitors. The runway, the single corridor, a rail network visitors
cannot use, the thin winter schedule and decision rights split across three tiers all sit outside a marketing
budget. The system has recovered; its constraints are now physical and institutional.
1.3 From recovery to growth
At the airport the recovery is complete. Gateway capacity now caps growth in the summer
peak, and demand and conversion cap it in the green season.
The limits of recovery logic
The action plan's task is to move the Western Cape from recovery to growth. At the airport the first half is
done. International passenger arrivals at Cape Town International reached 1.44m in FY24/25 against
1.16m in FY19/20, and 1.57m in FY25/26. On South African Tourism's provincial count the province still
stood below 2019 in 2025 (section 1.1).44
Recovery logic assumed capacity waiting to be refilled. Arrivals fell to 80,400 in FY20/21, and the task was
to refill idle seats, rooms and routes. That slack is gone. South Africa's international seat capacity is back
at pre-pandemic levels, and Cape Town International shares with Lanseria the highest load factor of the
country's five largest airports, 79% across all its seats in 2025.45
Cape Town did more than refill its seats. It added 17 mainline international routes between 2015 and
2025, more than any of eight peer airports, and the result is "a 'what next' moment". The rebound that a
recovery plan is built to capture has already happened.46
Seats in summer, demand in winter
Growth is now supply-constrained in the summer peak and demand-constrained in the green season,
when airlines withdraw seats. A survey of 34 outbound markets counts 97 million high-value, South Africa-
interested travellers, of whom only 1.4 million convert into arrivals. The conclusion is that "the challenge is
not demand generation but improving conversion into actual visits".47
The binding supply is peak capacity at the gateway. The growth model draws current air access as a line
at 1.8 million inbound seats, and the base case, in which Wesgro maintains current activity, reaches
1,802,929 arrivals at the 2030 plotted year. The chart below sets the base case against that line.48

Figure 1.4. Base case against the seat line
Source: Market Assessment Report, May 2026, p20 (base case by year from a 2025 base of 1,498,829; the 2028 to 2035 points are identical
in the Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p21). Seat line as drawn on the same slide. Airport relief from about 2032: Infrastructure Report, Jul
2026, p103. The model prints only these six year points and no seat table beneath the 1.8m line, so the crossing falls between 2028
(1,673,855) and 2030 (1,802,929). The conservative (1,822,634) and accelerated (2,516,282) cases are printed as 2035 end points only and
are not plotted.
The line is the model's assumption, not a measured cap. At the international routes' 76.0% load factor, 1.8
million inbound seats would carry about 1.37 million arrivals, fewer than the 1.57 million ACSA recorded in
FY25/26. The route table counts 3,816,797 international seats for the year to November 2025, more than
1.8 million each way even if that total counts both directions. What binds is the runway at peak hours in
the summer peak, at about 30 movements an hour. [Open item: ACSA or OAG to supply peak-hour and
seasonal slot headroom at Cape Town International.]49
The routes that exist are full. Eleven of the 27 international routes in the load-factor table ran above 80%
in the year to November 2025. Together they averaged 76.0% on the printed total, below the airport's 79%
across all seats. Twenty-nine international routes serve the airport in 2026. Five of the eleven are mature
long-haul routes, led by Newark at 88.6%, Atlanta at 84.3% and Frankfurt and London Heathrow at
83.0%.50
Full routes do not add seats of their own accord: "rather than increase capacity carriers will seek to
increase revenues via higher selling fares rather than add new services". Airlines also pull seats out of
winter: June carries 6.4% of the year's international capacity and December 10.8%.51
Behind the routes sits the runway. "The airport, not a shortage of visitors is what now caps Cape Town's
tourism growth". The single runway caps movements at about 30 an hour until relief arrives in about 2033
(section 1.2).52
The secondary gateways do not close the gap in time: "the certain fix is the latest, and the earliest big-
capacity option is the least certain". Route rights sit a tier up: air service agreements are a national
function. The Department of Economic Development rates restrictive bilateral agreements a high-impact
constraint on airlift.53

Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
Car-only regions and unbookable product
Beyond the Metro the constraint changes shape, not nature: accessibility falls from 4.7 in the Metro to 1.0
in the Central Karoo, and the visitor drives.54
Much of what the car can reach cannot be booked. Most rural properties have no OTA presence and
Route 62 has the lowest online bookability of the six touring routes. Even CapeNature takes about 70% of
its reservations through a call centre rather than its online platform.55
Growth above the norm
Global tourism grows 3% to 5% a year under stable conditions, the band the benchmarking work calls
organic growth. In 2024 the world grew 11% as recovery ran on, and only Chile, at 40.4%, and Singapore,
at 21%, grew far faster. The longer record is the better test (section 2.1). Santiago's airport grew from
about 16 million passengers in 2010 to more than 26 million in 2019, at least 5.5% a year.56
The Western Cape's two paths straddle the band. The base case implies 3.8% a year from 2025 to 2035
and the accelerated case 5.3%, and the accelerated case reverts to base "without confirmed routes".
Growth counts as structural only above that band, and only when it is "driven by deliberate changes to
demand drivers, access conditions or product systems, rather than cyclical recovery or short-term
promotion".57
Cape Town remains "the engine of the system, but growth cannot keep concentrating there". The
Department of Economic Development already reads the growth framing as overweight on volumes and
asks for a value and spend lens in place of arrivals. Growth now needs new markets, new seasons and
new regions, and each of those is a supply problem before it is a marketing problem.58
1.4 Approach and method
The study ran in four phases from November 2025, tested its hypothesis tree against the
evidence of each workstream and took each figure from a ranked hierarchy of sources.
Four phases
The study ran in four phases: inception for one month, demand-side analysis for four and supply-side
analysis for three. Enablement and final reporting ran for seven months, from May to the end of November
2026.59
Each phase had a distinct job. Inception aligned expectations and ways of working and produced the
Current State Diagnostic as a literature-based baseline. The demand phase covered source markets,
India and China route to market, benchmarks and trends; the supply phase covered spatial readiness and
community inclusion, and the two overlapped from February to April.60
The final phase built the M&E framework, consolidated the research into this report and prepared the
launch. Three mechanisms governed the work: fortnightly working sessions with Wesgro, Steering
Committee sessions at milestones and fortnightly written status updates.61

Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
The hypothesis tree
The analytical core is a hypothesis tree, in which each entry names a lever on growth and is framed so the
evidence could test it. Three pillars carry the tree: Reshape demand, Transform supply and Build enabling
capability. Each pillar holds two themes, and the six themes hold twenty focus areas [Summary of
Findings, 14 Oct 2026, pp15 to 16: deck not in hand; page citation to confirm]. Nineteen of the twenty
were tested against the evidence of a named workstream, and Appendix A1 traces each to the evidence
behind it.
The tree disciplined the evidence. The stakeholder work coded each of its 223 insight statements to a
pillar and a category of the tree before aggregating them. Because the study team did the coding, the
counts show where views cluster; they do not on their own confirm a focus area.62
The workstreams divide the tree between them. The demand and aviation work tested five of the six
demand focus areas and the case for routes and seats. The infrastructure work tested cruise, mobility,
product, visitor experience and capital. The inclusion work tested community inclusion and, with the
stakeholder and infrastructure work, bookability. The stakeholder engagement supplied most of the
evidence on climate risk, the flagship nature sites and the governance theme. The twentieth focus area,
workforce and service skills, has no workstream yet. The 300,000-jobs ambition has no labour analysis
behind it. Section 1.1 asks Wesgro and DEDAT to commission one in 2027.63
Source hierarchy
Every figure in this report carries a source and page, and sources rank in five tiers. Where tiers disagree
the higher tier wins; where two documents in one tier disagree, both values are recorded and the value
used is stated with the reason. Nothing is reconciled by averaging.
1. Interim deliverables, cited by the date printed on the cover and by PDF page index, since none
prints page numbers.
2. Working analyses: the Market Assessment Report of May 2026 and the three Skift memos on digital
and search insights (December 2025), segmentation (March 2026) and African markets (March
2026), cited by section.
3. Wesgro and government data cited through a deliverable: the Mero 2024 reports for all six districts,
the Pero 2025 and the Growth for Jobs Strategy 2023. The same tier holds the Tourism Blueprint,
Wesgro's five-year strategy and the StratEcon cruise liner economic contribution studies for 2023,
2024 and 2024/25.64
4. Benchmark and reference reports, such as the UN Tourism barometer behind the global growth
norm.65
5. External public sources, used only where no higher tier carries the figure and labelled as such.
Two conventions follow. The MIDAS Aviation Market Assessment and the Skift memos are Word files
without page numbers, so they are cited by section title. Summary material ranks below the deliverables:
where the two differ, the deliverable's figure is used and the difference is logged.
Interim deliverables
Nine dated documents carry the evidence. Appendix A9 dates each by its cover and gives the version in
hand and the SLA clauses it answers. Chapter 2 draws them together as one body of analysis in four
parts: the global benchmarks and their lessons (2.1), demand (2.2), supply (2.3) and the enabling
environment (2.4). The Inception Report frames 1.1 and 1.4, and the Current State Diagnostic frames 1.2.

Stakeholder engagement
Stakeholder engagement ran in four phases alongside the analysis. Introduction and framing ran from
January to March 2026, sense-making and validation in April and May, socialisation of draft
recommendations in June and July and mobilisation from August into October. The Stakeholder Insights
Report closed the first phase with insight tables for sixteen organisations, from Mossel Bay to the CTICC.
Sixteen is a count of those tables; the report prints no tally.67
The method was strict about what an insight is. Sessions were run, insights extracted, each coded to a
pillar and a category of the tree and then aggregated. The 223 statements are the project team's
paraphrases rated High, Medium or Low, not quotations, so this report attributes them to organisations
and never to named individuals.68
Two gaps in the record matter: phase 1 engaged no airline, online travel agent or tour operator, and the
six demand-side engagements were proposed for phase 2. [Open item: test the gateway and governance
chapters with ACSA, the Cape Town Air Access airline partners, the City of Cape Town and Cape Town
Tourism before Steerco.] The demand work drew instead on discussions with more than 20 stakeholders
of its own. The outcomes of engagement phases 2 to 4 sit in no document in hand [Letsema to supply the
phase 2 to 4 engagement record for Appendix A6].69
Data limits
Five limits in the data shape how the findings read. The headline arrivals series counts international
passenger arrivals at Cape Town International, not tourists, and Cape Town air arrivals stand proxy for the
province in the market shares. No approved methodology exists for provincial tourism GDP and jobs, and
no deliverable prints a Western Cape tourism employment baseline.70
The supply dataset behind the Infrastructure Report (Supply_Infrastructure.xlsx) holds no geography
below district, and its 17,195 records added after May 2026 are generated placeholders, as the study
team found when it checked the file. [Open item: confirm whether the Infrastructure Report's count of
50,332 assets includes those records; if it does, restate Tables 1.2, 2.4 and A.18 and the district shares
drawn from them.] The inclusion analysis runs on an OpenStreetMap asset layer, extracted on 16
September 2026, joined to Census 2011 sub-places, so its resident shares are fifteen years old.71
The growth model exists in two editions. The May Market Assessment runs from a 2025 base of
1,498,829 arrivals and the June Demand-Side Analysis from a 2024 base of 1,445,031. Both reach
2,175,778 in the base case and 2,516,282 in the accelerated case by 2035. The report runs the growth
case from the 2025 base, keeps FY24/25's 1.44m as the recovery baseline and states which is which
wherever both appear.72
Completion plan
A six-week completion plan agreed on 12 October 2026 governs the last stage of the work. [Summary of
Findings, 14 Oct 2026: six-week plan from 12 October, with weekly outputs and owners, to be inserted;
deck not in hand.] The report goes to Steerco, Exco and the Board before the public launch in February
2027.

1.5 How to read this report
The report is built so that a Board member can read the summary and any one focus area
on its own, and a programme owner can run the plan from Chapters 3 to 5.
Chapter map
The report moves from context to evidence to recommendations to delivery.
The executive summary tells the case in short: where the province stands, what caps growth and what 2.5
million arrivals would take. It then sets out the three shifts and the twenty focus areas, the first 24 months,
the headline indicators and the main risks. It stands alone, for the Board, Exco and the February 2027
launch audience. Chapter 1 is for every reader, and is the chapter of the body a Board member should
read if they read no other. It sets out what Wesgro asked for, where the system stands after recovery, why
the next step is constrained by peak capacity and how the study ran.
Chapter 2 sets out what the analysis found, in four parts: the global benchmarks and their lessons,
demand, supply and the enabling environment. It serves readers checking the evidence behind a Chapter
3 entry, and each part closes with a box naming the focus areas it supports. Chapter 3 takes the twenty
focus areas in turn. Each entry sets out the challenge or opportunity, the evidence, the course of action,
why it can work and the link to the plan. The Board and Exco can read it entry by entry and the
programme owner in full; section 3.4 gives the sequence.
Chapter 4 sets out which initiatives run, in what order, owned by whom, at what cost and with which
decision gates, for the programme owner, Steerco and the partners named in the RACI. Chapter 5 sets
out how progress is measured, through indicators, baselines, targets, data sources, the scorecard and the
2030 mid-term review, for Wesgro Research, the programme office and Steerco. Appendices A1 to A9
hold the traceability table, the demand model, the benchmark cases, readiness scores, the community fact
base, stakeholder inputs, charters, the toolkit and references, for analysts and anyone checking a figure.
Citations and figures
Sources sit in footnotes, one per paragraph, in the order the paragraph uses them. Each study document
is cited by a short title and page, in the form Demand-Side Analysis, p21; Appendix A9 gives each full title
and cover date. The executive summary carries no footnotes, because the chapters source its figures.
The page is the PDF page index counted from the cover, because none of the decks prints page numbers.
The MIDAS Aviation Market Assessment and the three Skift memos have no fixed pagination and are
cited by section title.
Where a deliverable's file carries a later date than its cover, Appendix A9 shows both.
Where two inputs disagree the report states the value it uses, names the other with its page and gives the
reason. The five-market share, printed as both 63% and 61% on one slide, is one case. Nothing is
averaged and nothing is reconciled silently.73
Section 1.1 sets out how the report counts arrivals; the 2.5 million goal is the accelerated case rounded.
Marks and codes
Highlighted text in square brackets marks an open item: a figure no input in hand carries, with the figure
needed and who holds it. Every page of this draft carries DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION in its header and the
cover carries a draft line; both come off at sign-off.

Demand-Side Analysis, p13.

Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
Three codes tie the chapters together. A Chapter 3 kicker names the entry's theme, 1.1 to 3.2, above the
focus area name as it reads on the tree [Summary of Findings, 14 Oct 2026, pp15 to 16: deck page
reference to be confirmed]; each entry closes with the initiative and indicator it links to. Initiatives in
Chapter 4 run I1 onwards in one sequence; indicators in Chapter 5 run T1-01 onwards for impact, T2-01
for outcome and T3-01 for output.
Appendix A1 holds the evidence behind the tree. For each focus area it records the workstream and
Chapter 2 sections behind it, rates the strength of the evidence and gives its initiative and indicator codes.
Any Chapter 3 claim can be followed back to a page and forward to an owner and a measure. Nine focus
areas rest on strong evidence and ten on adequate evidence. Two of the ten rest on indirect evidence:
focus area 5 has no fare or yield data, and focus area 15 is an inference from the pipeline tables. Focus
area 20 waits on a labour analysis still to be commissioned.

DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
Chapter 2
Analysis and insights
Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
Four parts build the case for the recommendations in Chapter 3. Section 2.1 draws lessons from
comparator destinations; sections 2.2, 2.3 and 2.4 then examine demand, supply and the enabling
environment, on which Pillars 1, 2 and 3 rest.
Demand is not what holds the province back. Its shape and the system that serves it do. Five markets and
six months carry most overseas visits, and seats into Cape Town run out at the peak. Beyond the Metro,
product is hard to reach and book, and in townships and rural settlements the missing piece is a local
seller. The institutions that should hold the system together have thinned. Section 2.1 first shows how the
fastest growers dealt with their own binding constraint.
Stakeholder evidence runs through sections 2.2 to 2.4. The first of four engagement phases ran from
January to March 2026. In it, 16 organisations from Mossel Bay to the CTICC gave 223 rated insights: 115
High, 102 Medium and 6 Low. Each stakeholder statement in this chapter is the project team's paraphrase
of a point an organisation made, attributed to that organisation and never to a person. [Open item: each
organisation named here to approve its paraphrase before publication.] Each part ends with a box, "What
this means for the focus areas", which links the part's evidence to each focus area it touches.74
2.1 Global benchmarks and their lessons
The destinations that grew fastest ran institutions, marketing and air access as one system
aimed at a single binding constraint. For the Western Cape that constraint is seats into one
gateway; in its townships and rural settlements it is the lack of a local seller.
Benchmarks test the 2.5 million goal against proven growth
The action plan aims at 2.5 million international arrivals by 2035, 5.3% a year on the accelerated path and
just above the world's norm (sections 1.1 and 1.3). Growth above trend is rare across whole regions;
where it happens, it is specific to a destination. Benchmarking asks which deliberate choices let
comparable destinations outgrow the world, and which of them the Western Cape can adapt. It looks for
how each destination removed what bound its growth, not for isolated good practice.75
The yardstick is the world's own growth. Growth of 3% to 5% a year is the norm under stable conditions;
above 5% marks structural acceleration and 10% or more a structural shift. UN Tourism puts global
growth at 11% in 2024, while recovery was still running, and about 4% in 2025. Growth counts as
structural only when it is "driven by deliberate changes to demand drivers, access conditions or product
systems, rather than cyclical recovery or short-term promotion". Each case was read for its demand
profile, two proven growth levers and how far they transfer to the Western Cape.76
Six comparators from a screen of 30
The screen scored 30 destinations across Africa, Europe, the Americas and Oceania against 12 criteria in
four groups. Market and access asks whether visitors arrive as the Western Cape's do, by long-haul air
through hubs from the same markets. Destination and product structure asks whether a gateway city
leads into several regions with a similar mix and price. Growth and inclusion compares growth stage,
seasonality, community participation and climate pressure; performance compares volume, yield and
infrastructure. Appendix A3 lists all twelve.77
Each destination received one average score across the twelve criteria, and the 30 were ranked on it.
Chile, Melbourne and Victoria, Valencia and Singapore came from the top of that ranking. Partial likeness

ruled out others in the upper half. Namibia lacks city, wine and cultural depth. Reykjavik and Iceland sell
one nature product with extreme seasonality. The Eastern Cape and Garden Route have no long-haul hub
or global brand role. The screen chose on likeness to the Western Cape, not on growth, so a comparator
can teach stability as well as speed.78
Score alone did not settle the set, which also had to be balanced and grounded in Africa. New Zealand,
ranked seventh at 3.67, is remote, long-haul and entered through one gateway, as the Western Cape is,
though its source markets and cruise trade differ. Kenya, ranked eleventh at 3.50, gives the set its African
case: a gateway city leading to coast, wildlife and culture, with long-haul reliance, regional dispersal and
community-based tourism. It is weaker on wine and agri-tourism and differs on price and governance, so it
is read for access, dispersal and inclusion rather than product.79
The six cover the constraints the province faces. Chile and New Zealand show how a gateway wins long-
haul seats and fills them; Singapore shows how hub traffic becomes visits, and Kenya how a gateway
carries visitors beyond its best-known product. Melbourne shows how events spread demand across the
year, and Valencia how quality is governed.

Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
Community cases and peer airports answer narrower questions
Five community cases ask what keeps tourism income in townships and rural settlements. Each matches
one of four types among the province's 107 long-listed communities. The types turn on two facts: whether
a community lies within 45 minutes of a tourism node, and whether it holds an attraction. Vilakazi Street in
Soweto and Santa Marta in Rio de Janeiro speak to communities near a node whose assets are
underused. Kliptown speaks to communities near a node with no attraction of their own. Comuna 13 in
Medellín, remote by access rather than distance, speaks to remote communities with assets, and Costa
Rica's rural network to remote communities without them.80
Cape Town's route record was set against eight peer airports: Calgary, Christchurch, Edinburgh, Keflavik,
Larnaca, Marrakech, Santiago and Valencia. Five are not their country's main airport, and most serve
seasonal or fast-growing inbound leisure markets. Santiago and Christchurch share Cape Town's remote
geography, and Santiago targets tripling its passenger volumes in twenty years.81
Cape Town wins more mainline routes than its peers
Between 2015 and 2025 Cape Town added 17 mainline international services, more than any of the eight
peer airports. Santiago added 14 services, Valencia 12 and Calgary 8. Yet winning routes is not the same
as holding seats through the winter, and the routes won now run full (section 2.3). Low-cost carriers tell a
different story: Edinburgh added 121 routes from 2015 to 2025, and low-cost carriers fly 81% of its routes.
At Cape Town, low-cost entry "would be a longer term opportunity". The Western Cape does not need to
learn how to win mainline routes. It needs what the leaders added: access funded as infrastructure and
marketing tied to each route.82
Fast growth in 2024 was partly recovery
Only Chile and Singapore grew far faster than the world in 2024: Chile by 40.4% and Singapore by 21%,
against the world's 11%. Neither rate proves structural growth. Singapore was still at 86% of its 2019
arrivals, and most of Chile's arrivals came overland, 40% of them from Argentina. Kenya, Valencia and
New Zealand grew only slightly faster than the world, and Melbourne's 4.3% is an Australia-wide forecast
rather than an actual. The lessons that follow come from what the leaders built over a decade, not from
one year's rate.83

The Western Cape should apply the same test to its own record: growth counts when deliberate change
drives it, not when recovery returns lost visitors.84

What each comparator faced and what it did
Chile built access first
Chile was chosen as a long-haul city gateway to coast, wine and wilderness, with Mediterranean seasons
like the Western Cape's. South America supplies 55% to 60% of its arrivals. Yet long-haul access was its
binding constraint, and Chile treated it as infrastructure. Santiago's airport traffic rose from about 16 million
passengers in 2010 to more than 26 million in 2019. A private concession won in 2015 funded about
US$900 million of expansion, under one of the world's most liberal aviation systems built on open-skies
agreements. Direct routes now link Santiago with Europe and Australia, and a domestic network carries
visitors on to Patagonia and the wine valleys. The lesson is blunt: "Tourism demand follows air access".85
84
Benchmarking, p8.
85
Benchmarking, p12, p22, p24, p59, p25, p23, p26; the source prints about 90% to 95% of 5.2 million international arrivals through Santiago,
which can hold only for air arrivals, since land borders carried 55.6% in 2024.
Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory 40
Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
New Zealand tied marketing to routes
New Zealand holds coast, wine, nature and culture within short travel distances, as the Western Cape
does. Air access economics bind its growth. It "shifted from awareness-driven marketing to route
accountable demand generation", tying budgets to specific long-haul corridors and designing campaigns
around airline breakeven and shoulder-season loads. The programme reports more than 16,000
incremental bookings and a return of about NZ$55 million in 2023/24. Government-backed early-stage
route incentives and data shared between tourism bodies and airlines support it. In the regions it curates
premium multi-day circuits and limits capacity to protect price.86
Singapore turned a hub into a destination
Singapore was chosen for joining air access, service quality, food culture and visitor segmentation in one
approach. Its question was how to turn transit through Changi into stays. Its answer is a stopover
programme, with the tourism board, Changi Airport Group and Singapore Airlines working "under a
coordinated strategy". It received 16.5 million international visitors in 2024 and record tourism receipts of
about S$29 billion. A hotel transformation map and fund lifted quality alongside, moving Singapore "from
lower-value stopover visits toward longer stays and higher-value tourism spending".87
Kenya made wildlife a governed system
Kenya is the African case, chosen for its long-haul reliance, regional dispersal and community-based
tourism. African markets supply 41% of its arrivals, while long-haul visitors spend more per head. Kenya
"institutionalised conservation as national economic infrastructure", joining wildlife protection, tourism and
community participation in one governed system. It positioned Nairobi as East Africa's aviation, diplomatic
and business hub, and its main airport now carries 68% of arrivals. Despite meaningful reforms,
fragmented investment and regulatory hurdles remain ongoing challenges. Kenya earned about US$1,460
per international arrival in 2024, against about US$890 for the Western Cape.88
Melbourne and Victoria fill the year with events
Melbourne plays the part for Victoria that Cape Town plays for the province: main point of entry, brand
anchor and engine of demand. Summer still brings the largest spike in international demand, so Victoria
curates a portfolio of events that "generate repeat, predictable demand spikes across the year". Behind
the calendar, hotel stock grew 21%, about 5,000 rooms. Melbourne "uses its event calendar to de-risk and
stimulate aviation demand". Its quietest months still hold 7% of arrivals and its busiest 12%, against 6.1%
and 12.7% for the Western Cape on the provisional 2025 shares. The gap is modest, so the lesson is less
a flatter curve than an events calendar airlines can plan seats around. Its case rests on stability and yield
rather than speed.89
Valencia governed quality across the destination
Valencia, a coastal city of culture and food, matches the province on climate, product mix and price. It
faced a familiar problem: fragmented governance and uneven quality across regions, operators and
seasons. It adopted a destination-wide quality system across its tourism businesses. Coordination sits in
"a permanent public-private structure led by Visit València", which joins municipal, provincial and regional
authorities in one decision-making system. It received 3.5 million international visitors in 2024, 13.9%
more than in 2023 and 24.4% above 2019. Spend rose faster still, by 21%.90
The leaders treat air access as infrastructure
The Western Cape faces both halves of the access problem Chile and New Zealand attacked: too few
seats at the peak and thin demand off it. On the growth model, the base case passes a line of 1.8 million

inbound seats by 2030. That line is the model's assumption; in practice the runway binds at peak hours
(section 2.3). Off-peak, June carries only 6.4% of the year's international capacity.91
The two cases point to three changes. Air access should be treated as "core economic infrastructure, not
a tourism add-on", with "a permanent, well-funded Public-Private Air Access Fund". The province should
"actively lobby for air service liberalisation and simplified visa regimes". Marketing budgets should be ring-
fenced around priority long-haul corridors, with an aviation partnership framework and early-stage route
support.92
Transit and events can be turned into demand
Singapore shows how a hub becomes a destination: its tourism board, airport and airline run one stopover
programme under a coordinated strategy. Cape Town's seven largest international routes all connect to
global hubs, so the same partnership with the hub carriers is open to it. Government, aviation, events and
industry should work to shared KPIs, linking air access, major events and joint marketing. City, provincial
and national actors should align around one gateway strategy, with MICE treated as a system lever.93
Events do the same work across the calendar, and the Western Cape has the green-season gap they
address (section 2.2). The province needs one event acquisition and calendar strategy across Wesgro,
the Convention Bureau, the City and the Province, with conference bidding aligned to sector strengths.94
Quality and value are governed, not encouraged
The leaders manage quality as a deliberate outcome. Valencia and Singapore set standards through
standing bodies rather than leaving them to operators. The results reported for Singapore include longer
stays and higher spend per visitor; those for Valencia include more consistent service, a stronger
reputation and more repeat visits.95
The cases ask the Western Cape first for a province-wide quality and standards framework led by
Wesgro. Within it would sit a provincial quality charter phased by district, with certification "visible across
booking platforms", alongside standing sector councils for wine, MICE, adventure and culture. The cases
also ask the province to measure its regions by yield rather than arrivals and to build conservation and
community inclusion into the offer.96
In communities, lasting sellers matter more than capital
In communities the binding constraint is the seller. The five cases show that local sellers outlast the
programmes that launch them. In Santa Marta in Rio de Janeiro, about 20 resident guides ran the tours by
2015 and outside jeep firms had left. The guides kept selling after the launch programme ended, while
assets and training without a route to market did not last. Access and safety were preconditions, not
product.97
Scale brings its own limits. Medellín's Comuna 13 drew 1.98 million visitors in 2025, 52% of them
international, with about 300 professional guides. Mobility built for residents became the stage, but
carrying-capacity limits came late. On Vilakazi Street in Soweto, ten restaurants employ more than 800
people. Public space upgrades amplify existing assets, but benefits concentrate on street-front owners.98
Neither capital nor donor money lasts without a seller that can stand alone. Costa Rica's community
network turned over more than USD 800,000 in 2013 and dissolved at the end of 2018; it did not survive

donor exit, though the record names no single cause. Kliptown in Soweto received a first allocation of
R436 million and was projected to draw almost 200,000 international visitors a year. By 2017 its museum
drew about 4,000 visitors a year, because capital built before an operator yields no operating income.99
The cases converge on one order: "a seller with a stake, a safe and workable visit, product that keeps
spend in the community, then exposure". In the Western Cape's communities, the seller therefore comes
before the asset (section 2.4). In Doringbaai and Elands Bay, a local entity comes before more product,
with its own selling channel and a succession plan that does not rest on a donor. Assets follow proven
demand.100
The system matters more than any lever
No single lever explains the leaders' growth. Sustained tourism growth "is not created by fragmented
interventions, but by a coordinated destination system in which governance, demand generation and
access reinforce one another". Chile's coordination works best "when anchored around a single strategic
constraint". The Western Cape's constraint has the same shape as Chile's long-haul access: seats into
one gateway airport (section 2.3).101
Yet coordination is hard everywhere. Victoria reports tension between national and state bodies over roles
and branding. Valencia struggles to join its data across institutions. The Western Cape's three tiers of
government show the same strain (section 2.4). The province should anchor its plan on one constraint, as
Chile did: peak seats into Cape Town first, one system built around them and, in the communities, a seller
before any asset.102
What the comparison cannot show
The cases show what worked elsewhere; they cannot prove it will work here. Singapore's tourism board
answers to one ministry, in a system strongly directed by the state yet closely tied to the private sector.
The Western Cape works across three tiers, and air rights and visas sit with national government (section
2.4). New Zealand's governance differs too, and transfer means adapting governance, data and capability
to local realities.103
The evidence also has gaps. No score by criterion is printed, and among the six only New Zealand (3.67)
and Kenya (3.50) have a printed score. The margins behind the choice cannot be checked. No case of
failure was studied. [Open item: add one comparator that underperformed, as the Inception Report's plan
asked (Inception Report, Nov 2025, p16).] Several comparator charts are unlabelled, so only printed
figures are quoted here, and Valencia's figures name no primary source. The recommended air access
fund carries no amount. None of the cases holds Western Cape data, so the province's side of each
comparison comes from sections 2.2 to 2.4. The six cases point the same way; the precise rates are
indicative.104

What this means for the focus areas
FA02, Create green-season demand with MICE and events. Melbourne's events-led system and
unified calendar.
FA04, Convert India and China hub traffic, and FA06, Build a regional foundation (Africa).
Singapore's joint strategy with its airport and airline; Kenya's hub strategy and African base.
FA05, Increase yield in mature markets, FA12, Balance impact on nature-based attractions,
and FA14, Manage climate risk and sustainable tourism. Yield-based metrics, capacity discipline
and conservation-led product.
FA07, Win direct routes and add seats on full routes, and FA15, Crowd in capital with targeted
investment strategies. Chile's airport concession and air access fund; New Zealand's route-tied
marketing.
FA10, Support diversified route and product development. Chile's regional portfolio; New
Zealand's premium circuits.
FA11, Align visitor experience in-destination, and FA03, Make the whole province findable and
bookable. Valencia's governed quality and certification visible on booking platforms.
FA13, Embed local destination bodies for community inclusion. Resident sellers outlasted the
programmes that launched them, and capital built before an operator, as in Kliptown, earned no
operating income.
FA16, Align institutional stakeholders across all three tiers, FA17, Strengthen industry and
government collaboration, and FA18, Expedite tourism-enabling policy and data sharing. One
coordinated system of governance, marketing and access, with shared data between tourism bodies
and airlines.
FA19, Lead removal of infrastructure, visa and safety deficits. Chile's open skies; safety before
product in the community cases.
FA20, Develop workforce and service skills. Singapore's hotel quality drive; the province's own
labour analysis is still to be commissioned.
2.2 Demand-side factors
Interest in the Western Cape is broad and growing. Yet demand is concentrated in five
markets and six months. The growth markets fly in through Gulf and African hubs and book
on platforms where Western Cape product is hard to find.
A 2025 values-based survey reached more than 25,000 travellers in 34 outbound markets, 17 of them
Wesgro priority markets, with 800 to 1,600 in each. More than 20 discussions with market-facing
stakeholders added judgement to desk review of Wesgro reports, Statistics South Africa and Google
search data.105
Five markets carry the province and pay among the most
The UK, Germany, the USA, the Netherlands and France supplied 63% of overseas arrivals in 2025, and
the first three alone 52%. The pattern is not new: the top five held 61% of Cape Town overseas air arrivals
from January to August 2024.106
The same markets pay among the most. Average spend per visitor in 2024 ran from R32,900 for the USA
and R26,500 for the UK down to R8,600 for Namibia and R8,500 for Zimbabwe. Only Nigerians and
105
Market Assessment, p40; Demand-Side Analysis, p7.
106
Demand-Side Analysis, p13; the slide prints 61% in its text, and 63% is the sum of its printed shares; Diagnostic, p29.
Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory 44
Unlocking Tourism Growth: An Action Plan for the Western Cape to 2035 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION
Ghanaians spend as much, from a far smaller base (below). High-spend visitors come from Europe and
North America, "tying future value growth to slower-growing source markets". The Western Cape must
therefore hold yield in its mature markets while volume growth comes from elsewhere.107
Six months carry three fifths of demand
Demand peaks in the southern summer and thins in winter. December, January and November took
12.7%, 11.5% and 11.3% of overseas visitors in 2025, while June fell to 6.1%. October to March hold
about 62% of the year. The 2024 Wesgro Visitor Tracking Survey gives a similar split of 61% and 39%.108
Growth therefore depends on "improving conversion in Apr-Sep rather than pushing incremental demand
into already busy peak months". Airlines cut seats in those same months, so filling the April to September
green season needs seats as well as conversion (section 2.3).109
Removing barriers, not demand, sets the upside
The scenarios differ by intervention, not by demand: "the difference between scenarios is not demand
potential, but the extent to which structural barriers are removed". The growth model builds each market's
rate from four levers: outbound growth, the Western Cape's penetration, the airlift ceiling and structural
conditions such as currency and visas. It anchors on OAG aviation data for access and traveller data for
demand.110
From the 2025 base, the base case reaches 2,175,778 arrivals by 2035, implied growth of 3.8% a year.
The conservative case reaches 1,822,634, at 2.0%, and the accelerated case 2,516,282, at 5.3%. The
base case's 3.8% sits within the 3% to 5% norm, and only the accelerated case clears the 5% mark of
structural acceleration (section 2.1).111

The step from 2,175,778 to 2,516,282 rests on confirmed direct routes to India and China, above-baseline
activation and more hub capacity into Cape Town. The dependency is hard: "without confirmed routes,
volume reverts to base". Those routes are not close. A direct Indian service is unlikely before 2030, and
China is a longer prize still. Until they come, the hub carriers carry the growth markets. Even the base
case passes the model's seat line at the 2030 year point (section 2.3).112
Hub markets add most arrivals, and India and China grow fastest
In the base case the hub markets add the most arrivals and the acceleration markets grow fastest. The
hub markets are the UAE, Ethiopia, Qatar and Turkey. The acceleration markets are the Gulf, China, India
and Brazil ([Open item: Skift to confirm which Gulf markets the acceleration group holds, since the UAE
and Qatar sit in the hub group.]). Between 2025 and 2035 hub markets add 206,584 arrivals, mature
markets 178,780, acceleration markets 175,818, regional markets 110,298 and Japan and South Korea
5,469.113

The right segments can spread demand
Four values-based segments fit the Western Cape, together about 97 million addressable travellers. Each
has priority markets: Curious Explorers in the UK and the USA, and Creative Connectors in India.
Discerning Connoisseurs are paired with China and the UAE, and Reflective Adventurers with Germany.115

Curious Explorers and Discerning Connoisseurs are "most aligned to the Western Cape's offering". The
segments also matter for spread, not only for volume. Curious Explorers offer the "strongest opportunity
for geographic dispersal beyond Cape Town"; Reflective Adventurers have "strong potential to support
seasonality mitigation" through year-round outdoor and wellness travel. These two are the segments to
target for the regions and the green season.116
India and China already arrive through hubs
The growth markets are already in the air, on other carriers. About 71,000 Indian travellers and about
30,000 Chinese reach Cape Town each year through connecting hubs, together about 101,000 journeys.
Addis Ababa and Dubai carry 81% of the Indian travellers, and half of them start in Mumbai. Direct Indian
service is "unlikely before 2030 due to aircraft constraints at Air India and IndiGo". For China, a China
Southern service from Guangzhou is the likeliest first direct route.117
Australia (61,000 indirect travellers) and Saudi Arabia (137,000 passengers, with Riyadh Air's planned
hub) come next. The Western Cape should grow this hub traffic now. Singapore shows the method: its
tourism board, airport and airline work to one strategy (section 2.1). Here the partners are the hub carriers:
Emirates, Ethiopian, Qatar Airways and Turkish Airlines. Wesgro should build early relationships with the
Indian carriers and Indian government authorities. It should treat China as a long-term investment in
awareness and indirect volume before any non-stop service.118
Each market books on its own platforms
Conversion happens on platforms that differ by market. Chinese travellers discover on Douyin and
Xiaohongshu and book on Trip.com, Qunar and Fliggy. Indian travellers book through MakeMyTrip,
Booking.com and Expedia, and much Gulf travel still runs through private advisors and concierge
agencies.119
